		<style id="wp-custom-css">
			.jr-link-button.dashicons-before:before,
.jr-icon-button.dashicons-before:before {
	display: none;
	content: '';
}
@media (min-width: 922px) {
.header-main-layout-1 .main-header-container {
  align-items: center;
}
}

@media only screen and (max-width: 600px) {
.entry-content figure.alignleft,
.entry-content img.alignleft, .entry-content figure.alignright,
.entry-content img.alignright {
	float:none;
	display:block;
	margin-left:auto;
	margin-right:auto;
  }
	
.entry-content .wp-block-image .aligncenter>figcaption,
.entry-content .wp-block-image .alignleft>figcaption, 
	.entry-content .wp-block-image .alignright>figcaption {
		display: block;
	}
}

.ast-above-header-navigation .menu-item.jr-donate-link .menu-link,
.jr-donate-link a {
	color: #ffffff;
	border: 1px solid #AA1F24;
	background-color: #AA1F24;
	padding: .25rem .5rem;
	text-transform: uppercase;
}

.ast-above-header-navigation .menu-item.jr-donate-link a:hover,
.ast-above-header-navigation .menu-item.jr-donate-link a:focus,
.jr-donate-link a:hover,
.jr-donate-link a:focus {
	color: #AA1F24;
	background-color: #fff;
}

.ast-header-break-point .ast-above-header-menu-items .menu-item.jr-donate-link .menu-link:hover,
.ast-header-break-point .ast-above-header-menu-items .menu-item.jr-donate-link .menu-link:focus {
	border: 1px solid #AA1F24;
}

.jr-social-follow > .menu-link,
.ast-above-header-navigation .jr-social-follow.menu-item > .menu-link {
	color: unset;
}

.ast-header-break-point .toggle-on .ast-above-header-menu-items {
	display: flex;
	flex-wrap: wrap;
}
/* Stretch the About Us menu item on small screens */
.ast-header-break-point .ast-above-header-menu-items .menu-item:first-of-type {
	width: 100%;
}

.ast-header-break-point .ast-above-header-menu-items .menu-item.jr-social-follow {
	flex: 0 1 auto;
}
/* Stretch the Donate menu item on small screens */
.ast-header-break-point .jr-donate-link {
    flex: none;
    text-align: center;
		width: 100%;
}

/* Allow menu to be full width until new breakpoint */
@media (max-width: 1040px) {
.header-main-layout-1 .main-header-bar-navigation {max-width: calc(100% + 4rem);}}	


.ast-header-break-point .ast-above-header-menu-items .menu-item.jr-donate-link .menu-link {
    padding: .5rem;
}

.ast-header-break-point .main-header-bar-navigation li, .ast-header-break-point .main-header-bar-navigation .menu-item .menu-link, .ast-header-break-point .main-header-bar-navigation .ast-above-header-menu-items .menu-item .menu-link, .ast-header-break-point .main-header-bar-navigation .ast-below-header-menu-items .menu-item .menu-link {
    padding-left: 0;
}

.above-header-nav-padding-support .ast-justify-content-flex-end .ast-above-header-menu>.jr-donate-link.menu-item:last-child .menu-link {
	padding-right: 0.5rem;
}
.ast-header-break-point .ast-above-header-menu-items .menu-item.jr-donate-link .menu-link {
	padding: .5rem;
}

.ast-header-break-point .main-header-bar-navigation ul.ast-above-header-menu-items,
.ast-header-break-point .main-header-bar-navigation ul.ast-below-header-menu-items {
	padding-right: 2rem;
}		</style>

		<header class="entry-header ">

			
			<div class="entry-category-meta"><span class="cat-links"><a class="Media" href="https://journalistsresource.org/media/" rel="category tag">Media</a></span></div><h1 class="entry-title" itemprop="headline">Trauma-informed journalism: What it is, why it&#8217;s important and tips for practicing it</h1>			<p class="entry-subtitle">Experts and journalists who have researched and worked with trauma survivors say that practicing trauma-informed journalism not only leads to better, more accurate stories, but also helps protect survivors from further harm.  </p>
		<div class="entry-meta">by <span class="posted-by vcard author" itemtype="https://schema.org/Person" itemscope="itemscope" itemprop="author">		<a href="https://journalistsresource.org/author/naseemmiller/" title="Posts by Naseem S. Miller" rel="author">Naseem S. Miller</a>		</span>

		 | <span class="posted-on"><span class="published" itemprop="datePublished"> April 13, 2022 </span></span> | <span class="ast-terms-link"><a href="https://journalistsresource.org/tag/covering-trauma/" class="">covering trauma</a>, <a href="https://journalistsresource.org/tag/journalism-tips/" class="">journalism tips</a>, <a href="https://journalistsresource.org/tag/mental-health/" class="">mental health</a>, <a href="https://journalistsresource.org/tag/trauma/" class="">trauma</a></span></div><div class="post-thumb-img-content post-thumb"><img width="860" height="466" src="https://journalistsresource.org/wp-content/uploads/2022/04/IMG_1700-860x466.jpg" class="attachment-860x466 size-860x466" alt="trauma-informed journalism and tragedies" itemprop="image" decoding="async" loading="lazy" srcset="https://journalistsresource.org/wp-content/uploads/2022/04/IMG_1700-scaled-860x466.jpg 860w, https://journalistsresource.org/wp-content/uploads/2022/04/IMG_1700-scaled-921x500.jpg 921w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 860px) 100vw, 860px" /></div><div class="entry-thumb-caption">A makeshift memorial in Orlando, Florida, after the Pulse nightclub mass shooting on June 12, 2016. (Naseem S. Miller)</div>
			
		</header><!-- .entry-header -->

	
	
	<div class="entry-content clear"
	itemprop="text"	>

						<div id="republication-tracker-tool-modal-content" style="display:none;"><button  class="republication-tracker-tool-close"><span class="screen-reader-text">Close window</span><span class="republication-tracker-tool-close-icon"></span></button><h2 id="republish-modal-label">Republish this article</h2><div class="cc-policy"><div class="cc-license"><a rel="noreferrer license" target="_blank" href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nd/4.0/"><img decoding="async" alt="Creative Commons Attribution-NoDerivatives 4.0 International License" style="border-width:0" src="https://licensebuttons.net/l/by-nd/4.0/88x31.png" /></a><p>This work is licensed under a <a rel="noreferrer license" target="_blank" href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nd/4.0/">Creative Commons Attribution-NoDerivatives 4.0 International License</a>.</p>
</div><p><i style="color: #222222;font-family: Arial, Helvetica, sans-serif;font-size: small"><span style="font-size: 10pt;font-family: Helvetica, sans-serif;color: #383838">You are free to republish this piece both online and in print, and we encourage you to do so with the embed code provided below. We only ask that you follow <b><a href="https://journalistsresource.org/republishing-guidelines/">a few basic guidelines</a>.</b>  </span></i></p></div><div class="article-info"><h1>Trauma-informed journalism: What it is, why it&#8217;s important and tips for practicing it</h1>
<p class="byline">by Naseem S. Miller, The Journalist&#039;s Resource <br />April 13, 2022</p>
</div>			<div class="republication-content-section">
				
				<div class="republish-content-container">
					<textarea
						id="republication-tracker-tool-shareable-content"
						class="republish-content__textarea "
						data-tab-content="html"
						readonly
						rows="5"
						aria-label="Republish this article (HTML format)"
					>&lt;h1&gt;Trauma-informed journalism: What it is, why it&#8217;s important and tips for practicing it&lt;/h1&gt;
&lt;p class=&quot;byline&quot;&gt;by Naseem S. Miller, The Journalist&#039;s Resource &lt;br /&gt;April 13, 2022&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;p&gt;You may have heard the term "trauma-informed" in the context of health care, education and, more recently, journalism. In general, being trauma-informed means recognizing that trauma is common and people may have experienced serious trauma at some point in their lives.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Trauma-informed practices first took shape in medicine, after the medical community began to understand trauma and post-traumatic stress disorder.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Other fields, such as &lt;a href="https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.3102/0091732X18821123" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;education&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://ncler.acl.gov/files/trauma-informed-lawyering.aspx#:~:text=A%20trauma%2Dinformed%20legal%20practice,their%20clients%20and%20improve%20advocacy." target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;law&lt;/a&gt;, have also been discussing trauma-informed practices.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;“Trauma-informed journalism” is a relatively new term, even though covering trauma -- ranging from storms and fires to sexual assaults and homicides to mass shootings and wars -- has always been a part of journalists' work.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Trauma-informed journalism can mean different things to different people, says Tamara Cherry, who worked as a crime reporter for nearly 15 years and is the author of upcoming book, "The Trauma Beat: Victims, Survivors &amp;amp; the Journalists Who Tell Their Stories," slated for publication in Spring 2023.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;"Trauma-informed journalism means understanding trauma, understanding what a trauma survivor is experiencing before you show up at their door, and understanding how your actions [as a journalist] will impact them after you pack up and leave," says Cherry. "It's also about creating the safe and predictable spaces. It's about forgetting all the rules that we usually abide by when we're interviewing school board officials and politicians and recognizing that when it comes to trauma, we need to be treating our interview subjects differently."&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Discussions about trauma-informed journalism are becoming more common due to a confluence of factors, including the COVID-19 pandemic and an increased hostility toward journalists. Together, these elements "have really made it clear that for journalists to do their jobs, they need to understand trauma more, so that they can tell better stories," says &lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/about/people/elana-newman" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Elana Newman&lt;/a&gt;, research director at the Dart Center for Journalism &amp;amp; Trauma at Columbia University and McFarlin Professor of Psychology at the University of Tulsa.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This explainer and tip sheet on trauma-informed journalism is based on a review of several reliable sources on trauma-informed reporting and interviews with Newman of the Dart Center, and Cherry, founder of Pickup Communications PR agency, which provides services and tools for victims and survivors of traumatic events, the journalists who cover their stories and the criminal justice sector. We use&lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/resources/dart-center-style-guide"&gt; The Dart Center Style Guide for Trauma-Informed Journalism&lt;/a&gt; as our source for defining trauma-related terminology.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;blockquote class="wp-block-quote"&gt;
&lt;p&gt;"Trauma-informed journalism means understanding trauma, understanding what a trauma survivor is experiencing before you show up at their door, and understanding how your actions [as a journalist] will impact them after you pack up and leave."&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt; &lt;cite&gt;Tamara Cherry&lt;/cite&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/blockquote&gt;
&lt;h3 class="wp-block-heading"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;1. Understand that trauma impacts the brain, including the memory and the ability to vocalize events as they happened.&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Trauma results from an event, series of events, or set of circumstances that are physically or emotionally harmful or life threatening to an individual, and have "lasting adverse effects on the individual’s functioning and mental, physical, social, emotional, or spiritual well-being," according to the &lt;a href="https://www.samhsa.gov/trauma-violence" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;U.S. Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Trauma can significantly affect a person's perception and judgment. When faced with traumatic situations, the brain can go into survival mode and people can suddenly feel and behave differently from their normal selves. "That’s why eyewitnesses to traumatic events are notoriously unreliable. It’s also why even seasoned journalists at moments of high emotion can sometimes get it wrong," according to the Dart Center's "&lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/sites/default/files/DCE_JournoTraumaHandbook.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Trauma &amp;amp; Journalism: A Guide for Journalists, Editors &amp;amp; Managers&lt;/a&gt;."&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Trauma impacts the brain, memories and a person's ability to vocalize their stories, says Cherry. Immediately after a tragedy, "we as journalists are asking people to tell their stories, when quite often they literally can't," she says. "So, we might think that we're getting their story, but really, we're getting whatever version of their story that their brain is coming up with in the moment."&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;But months down the road, when the sources have had time to heal a little and they're not in &lt;a href="https://www.health.harvard.edu/staying-healthy/understanding-the-stress-response"&gt;fight or flight&lt;/a&gt; mode, we might actually get a more accurate story, Cherry says.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 class="wp-block-heading"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;2. When getting informed consent to tell a trauma survivor’s story, err on the side of overexplaining.&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In the context of trauma-informed reporting, informed consent means informing your sources about the nature of your story and having their consent to use their name and report on their experiences. While this is an important aspect of reporting many stories, trauma-informed reporting requires journalists to err on the side of overexplaining. &amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;"Don’t assume [sources] know what terms like ‘on the record’ or ‘on background’ mean. If that’s something you’re discussing, make sure to be extremely clear about how they will or won’t be identified," writes journalist &lt;a href="https://twitter.com/Alice_Wilder"&gt;Alice Wilder&lt;/a&gt; in "&lt;a href="https://transom.org/2021/trauma-informed-reporting/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Trauma Informed Reporting&lt;/a&gt;," published for &lt;a href="http://transom.org/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Transom&lt;/a&gt;, a nonprofit organization that brings new voices and ideas to public broadcasting via workshops and its website.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Give your sources the option to review their quotes or even to change their mind about being quoted before a story is published.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In writing her book, Cherry allowed the people she surveyed and interviewed -- including more than 100 trauma survivors -- to review the parts in which they were quoted or where their stories were used to ensure their continued consent and accuracy of the survivors’ accounts after her initial interview.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;"I knew enough about trauma to really appreciate the weight of responsibility that I had to get this right and to not cause further harm,” she says. “I wanted them to be able to review their excerpt, but I didn't want it to leave them in a dark place. I wanted it to leave them in a better place.”&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;blockquote class="wp-block-quote"&gt;
&lt;p&gt;"Don’t assume [sources] know what terms like ‘on the record’ or ‘on background’ mean. If that’s something you’re discussing, make sure to be extremely clear about how they will or won’t be identified."&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt; &lt;cite&gt;Alice Wilder&lt;/cite&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/blockquote&gt;
&lt;h3 class="wp-block-heading"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;3. Give power to survivors during the interview and storytelling process.&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Journalists are trained not to let sources pick which questions they want to answer, see the questions ahead of the interview or the story before it's published. But when it comes to interviewing survivors of trauma, it's OK to break those rules. Let your sources know that they have control of the situation.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;"One simple way to raise their comfort level is by not pushing any questions that they are not prepared to answer," writes Maggie Doheny in her article "&lt;a href="https://rjionline.org/news/best-practices-for-trauma-informed-journalism/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Best Practices for Trauma-Informed Journalism&lt;/a&gt;," published by the Reynolds Journalism Institute in December 2021.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The Dart Center's guide, "&lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/content/tragedies-journalists-6?section=all" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Tragedies &amp;amp; Journalists&lt;/a&gt;," has five tips for interviewing victims of trauma:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
&lt;li&gt;Always treat victims with dignity and respect.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;Clearly identify yourself.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;Never say "I understand" or "I know how you feel."&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;Don't overwhelm your source with the hardest questions first.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;If you receive a harsh reaction, leave a number or your card and explain that survivors can contact you if they want to talk later.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In the aftermath of a tragedy, prepare yourself for a wide range of responses from survivors. "Bear in mind the emotional impact of what has happened. Approach people with care, respect and kindness. Take a moment to introduce yourself, make eye contact and explain why you would like to talk to them. Take it slowly and don’t rush -- however chaotic the circumstances. Don’t just stuff a microphone in someone’s face and expect an interview," according to the Dart Center's &lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/sites/default/files/DCE_JournoTraumaHandbook.pdf"&gt;trauma and journalism guide&lt;/a&gt;. &amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;“Never ask that most overused and least effective of journalistic questions: ‘How do you feel?’ You may get tears in response, but you’re not likely to get a coherent, useful or meaningful answer. ‘How do you feel?’ is the one question survivors and victims consistently say they find the most distressing and inappropriate. Better options include: ‘How are you now?’ or ‘How did you experience that?’ or ‘What do you think about …?’” according to the Dart Center’s &lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/sites/default/files/DCE_JournoTraumaHandbook.pdf"&gt;trauma and journalism guide&lt;/a&gt;. &amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;"Opt for open-ended questions and let subjects know that they’re not required to answer questions," advises Wilder in her &lt;a href="https://transom.org/2021/trauma-informed-reporting/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Transom article&lt;/a&gt;. "Instead of 'Start at the beginning' or other chronological based questions ask, 'Where would you like to begin?' or 'Would you tell me what you are able to remember about your experience?'"&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Wilder also advises against questioning&amp;nbsp;why&amp;nbsp;your source is emotional: "Even if the traumatic event was long ago, or if it doesn’t seem 'that bad' to you, their reaction should be respected," she write. She also advises against saying “I understand what you’re going through” or “I know how you feel.”&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Ask survivors what they would like to achieve in telling their story, advises Cherry.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;And if you're producing a podcast, video, or a printed story about a crime or tragedy that happened in the past, make sure the survivors are aware of it and are extended the opportunity to include their voice and be part of the process. "Because if they're not, then they could really feel like we are talking about them behind their back," says Cherry. "Or they can be surprised by it because they might not even know [the podcast/show/story] is happening."&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;blockquote class="wp-block-quote"&gt;
&lt;p&gt;"‘How do you feel?’ is the one question survivors and victims consistently say they find the most distressing and inappropriate. Better options include: ‘How are you now?’ or ‘How did you experience that?’ or ‘What do you think about …?'"&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt; &lt;cite&gt;Dart Center's &lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/sites/default/files/DCE_JournoTraumaHandbook.pdf"&gt;Trauma &amp;amp; Journalism: A Guide For Journalists, Editors &amp;amp; Managers&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/cite&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/blockquote&gt;
&lt;h3 class="wp-block-heading"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;4. Have a plan for your interviews.&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If you're interviewing a trauma survivor, think about how you're caring for them before, during and after the interview.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Let survivors pick the place of the interview, so they can feel safe. Tell survivors what questions you're planning to ask, Cherry writes in her &lt;a href="https://www.pickupcommunications.com/tips" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;tip sheet&lt;/a&gt;. Ask if there are questions you should avoid. &lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;During the interview, make sure survivors aren't reliving the event. Find out if they want to take a break. &lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;At then end of the interview, thank your sources and let them how their interview will be used and when to expect the story, advises journalist and author Jo Healey in "&lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/resources/reporting-coronavirus-handling-sensitive-remote-interviews" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Reporting on Coronavirus: Handling Sensitive Remote Interviews&lt;/a&gt;," published in 2020 on the Dart Center's website.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 class="wp-block-heading"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;5. Don't forget about your own mental well-being.&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;figure class="wp-block-embed alignright is-type-wp-embed is-provider-the-journalist-039-s-resource wp-block-embed-the-journalist-039-s-resource"&gt;
&lt;div class="wp-block-embed__wrapper"&gt;
https://journalistsresource.org/home/self-care-tips-for-journalists-plus-a-list-of-several-resources/
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;/figure&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Over the years, research has shown that journalists' job can &lt;a href="https://journalistsresource.org/environment/job-stress-journalists-health-research/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;affect their mental health&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Depending on their beats or work locations,&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/epdf/10.1023/A%3A1024405716529" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;4%&lt;/a&gt;&amp;nbsp;to&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/content/covering-trauma-impact-on-journalists" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;59%&lt;/a&gt;&amp;nbsp;of journalists have symptoms of post-traumatic stress disorder, or PTSD, according to the&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/content/covering-trauma-impact-on-journalists" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Dart Center&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Journalists should be aware of signs of trouble, which include inability to concentrate, feeling on edge or numb, or inability to feel compassion for sources, Newman explained in &lt;a href="https://journalistsresource.org/home/self-care-tips-for-journalists-plus-a-list-of-several-resources/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;a 2021&lt;/a&gt;. Other signs include inability to sleep, feeling angry, or excessive use of alcohol.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Here are five self-care tips from the Dart Center's 2009 guide, "&lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/content/tragedies-journalists-6?section=all" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Tragedies &amp;amp; Journalists&lt;/a&gt;":&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
&lt;li&gt;Know your limits.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;Take breaks.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;Find a friend or colleague who is a good listener.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;Find a hobby, exercise, spend time with family and friends.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;Seek counseling if you feel overwhelmed.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Newman also shared self-care tips for journalists in 2021, which we summarized in "&lt;a href="https://journalistsresource.org/home/self-care-tips-for-journalists-plus-a-list-of-several-resources/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Self-care tips for journalists -- plus a list of several resources&lt;/a&gt;."&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 class="wp-block-heading"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Recommended reading&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;figure class="wp-block-embed alignright is-type-wp-embed is-provider-the-journalist-039-s-resource wp-block-embed-the-journalist-039-s-resource"&gt;
&lt;div class="wp-block-embed__wrapper"&gt;
https://journalistsresource.org/home/journalism-stress-solutions/
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;/figure&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/resources/dart-center-style-guide"&gt;The Dart Center Style Guide for Trauma-Informed Journalism&lt;/a&gt;," Dart Center for Journalism &amp;amp; Trauma, June 2021.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/sites/default/files/DCE_JournoTraumaHandbook.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Trauma &amp;amp; Journalism: A Guide for Journalists, Editors &amp;amp; Managers&lt;/a&gt;," Dart Center, 2007.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/content/tragedies-journalists-6?section=all" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Tragedies &amp;amp; Journalists&lt;/a&gt;," a 40-page guide to help journalists, photojournalists and editors report on violence while protecting both victims and themselves. Dart Center, February 2009.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://www.pickupcommunications.com/tips" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Trauma-Informed Journalism: Tips for Telling Important Stories, Better&lt;/a&gt;," Tamara Cherry. April 2022.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://transom.org/2021/trauma-informed-reporting/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Trauma Informed Reporting&lt;/a&gt;," Alice Wilder. &lt;a href="http://transom.org/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Transom.org&lt;/a&gt;, March 2021.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/resources/trauma-informed-interviewing-techniques-clinician%E2%80%99s-toolkit" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Trauma-Informed Interviewing: Techniques from a Clinician’s Toolkit&lt;/a&gt;," Kate Porterfield. Dart Center for Journalism &amp;amp; Trauma, November 2019.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://rjionline.org/news/best-practices-for-trauma-informed-journalism/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Best practices for trauma-informed journalism&lt;/a&gt;," Maggie Doheny. Donald W. Reynolds Journalism Institute at the University of Missouri, December 2021.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=leVUQs18BQc" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;A training session on trauma-informed reporting&lt;/a&gt;," &lt;a href="https://nicolefroio.com/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Nicole Froio&lt;/a&gt;. Hosted by The International journalists' Network, December 2021.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://centerforhealthjournalism.org/resources/lessons/keep-these-seven-lessons-mind-when-interviewing-trauma-survivors" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Keep these seven lessons in mind when interviewing trauma survivors&lt;/a&gt;," Lilly Dayton. Center for Health Journalism.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://www.amazon.com/Trauma-Reporting-Journalists-Covering-Sensitive-ebook/dp/B07S1PC8V3/ref=sr_1_1?crid=2C62D5WZIMCN3&amp;amp;keywords=Trauma+Reporting%3A+A+Journalist%27s+Guide+to+Covering+Sensitive+Stories&amp;amp;qid=1649359995&amp;amp;sprefix=%2Caps%2C754&amp;amp;sr=8-1" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Trauma Reporting: A Journalist's Guide to Covering Sensitive Stories&lt;/a&gt;," &lt;a href="https://traumareporting.com/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Jo Healey&lt;/a&gt;, July 2019.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/content/weathering-trauma-storms-training-journalists-in-interviewing-technique" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Weathering the Trauma Storms: Developing Interviewing Techniques&lt;/a&gt;," by Gavin Rees. Dart Center, June 2007.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/content/covering-children-trauma" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Covering Children and Trauma&lt;/a&gt;," Ruth Teichroeb. Dart Center, February 2009.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/events/2019/09/resources-reporting-refugees-and-migration-through-eyes-young-children" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Resources: Reporting on Refugees and Migration Through the Eyes of Young Children&lt;/a&gt;," Dart Center, September 2019.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://www.coveringcrsv.org/"&gt;Reporting on Sexual Violence in Conflict&lt;/a&gt;," Dart Centre Europe.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://centerforhealthjournalism.org/resources/lessons/how-working-sexual-assault-survivors-changed-way-i-think-about-journalism" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;How working with sexual assault survivors changed the way I think about journalism&lt;/a&gt;," Samantha Caiola. Center for Health Journalism.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/content/working-with-traumatic-imagery" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Working with Traumatic Imagery&lt;/a&gt;," Dart Center, August 2014.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/resources/reporting-coronavirus-handling-sensitive-remote-interviews" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Reporting on coronavirus: Handling sensitive remote interviews&lt;/a&gt;," &lt;a href="https://traumareporting.com/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Jo Healey&lt;/a&gt;. Dart Center, April 2020.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://traumastewardship.com/inside-the-book/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Trauma Stewardship: An Everyday Guide to Caring for Self While Caring for Others&lt;/a&gt;" by Laura van Dernoot Lipsky and Connie Burk, May 2009.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://firstdraftnews.org/wp-content/uploads/2017/04/vicarioustrauma.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Journalism and Vicarious Trauma: A Guide for Journalists, Editors and News Organizations&lt;/a&gt;," by Sam Dubberley and Michele Grant. &lt;a href="https://firstdraftnews.org/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;First Draft&lt;/a&gt;, April 2017.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://dartcenter.org/resources/journalists-coping-trauma"&gt;Resources for Journalists Coping With Trauma," Dart Center, June 2021.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;"&lt;a href="https://www.amazon.ca/Body-Keeps-Score-Healing-Trauma/dp/0143127748/ref=asc_df_0143127748/?hvadid=309622281255&amp;amp;hvdev=c&amp;amp;hvdvcmdl=&amp;amp;hvlocint=&amp;amp;hvlocphy=9001255&amp;amp;hvnetw=g&amp;amp;hvpone=&amp;amp;hvpos=&amp;amp;hvptwo=&amp;amp;hvqmt=&amp;amp;hvrand=1417875730307083549&amp;amp;hvtargid=pla-434690707169&amp;amp;linkCode=df0&amp;amp;psc=1&amp;amp;tag=googleshopc0c-20" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;The Body Keeps the Score&lt;/a&gt;" by &lt;a href="https://www.besselvanderkolk.com/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Dr. Bessel van der Kolk&lt;/a&gt;, September 2015.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.poynter.org/shop/ethics/journalism-and-trauma/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"&gt;Journalism and Trauma&lt;/a&gt;, a free course on how traumatic stress affects victims and how to interview trauma victims with compassion and respect, offered by Poynter.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This &amp;lt;a target=&amp;quot;_blank&amp;quot; href=&amp;quot;https://journalistsresource.org/home/trauma-informed-journalism-explainer/&amp;quot;&amp;gt;article&amp;lt;/a&amp;gt; first appeared on &amp;lt;a target=&amp;quot;_blank&amp;quot; href=&amp;quot;https://journalistsresource.org&amp;quot;&amp;gt;The Journalist&amp;#039;s Resource&amp;lt;/a&amp;gt; and is republished here under a &amp;lt;a target=&amp;quot;_blank&amp;quot; href=&amp;quot;https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nd/4.0/&amp;quot;&amp;gt;Creative Commons Attribution-NoDerivatives 4.0 International License&amp;lt;/a&amp;gt;.&amp;lt;img src=&amp;quot;https://journalistsresource.org/wp-content/uploads/2020/11/cropped-jr-favicon-150x150.png&amp;quot; style=&amp;quot;width:1em;height:1em;margin-left:10px;&amp;quot;&amp;gt;&lt;/p&gt;
</textarea>

									</div>
			</div>
						<button onclick="ClipboardUtils.copyFromElement( getActiveTextarea(), this )" class="republication-tracker-tool__copy-button republication-tracker-tool__copy-button--main show-for-html">Copy to Clipboard</button>
				</div>1					</div>
				</div>
<p>You may have heard the term &#8220;trauma-informed&#8221; in the context of health care, education and, more recently, journalism. In general, being trauma-informed means recognizing that trauma is common and people may have experienced serious trauma at some point in their lives.</p>



<p>Trauma-informed practices first took shape in medicine, after the medical community began to understand trauma and post-traumatic stress disorder.</p>



<p>Other fields, such as <a href="https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.3102/0091732X18821123" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">education</a> and <a href="https://ncler.acl.gov/files/trauma-informed-lawyering.aspx#:~:text=A%20trauma%2Dinformed%20legal%20practice,their%20clients%20and%20improve%20advocacy." target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">law</a>, have also been discussing trauma-informed practices.</p>



<p>“Trauma-informed journalism” is a relatively new term, even though covering trauma &#8212; ranging from storms and fires to sexual assaults and homicides to mass shootings and wars &#8212; has always been a part of journalists&#8217; work.</p>



<p>Trauma-informed journalism can mean different things to different people, says Tamara Cherry, who worked as a crime reporter for nearly 15 years and is the author of upcoming book, &#8220;The Trauma Beat: Victims, Survivors &amp; the Journalists Who Tell Their Stories,&#8221; slated for publication in Spring 2023.</p>



<p>&#8220;Trauma-informed journalism means understanding trauma, understanding what a trauma survivor is experiencing before you show up at their door, and understanding how your actions [as a journalist] will impact them after you pack up and leave,&#8221; says Cherry. &#8220;It&#8217;s also about creating the safe and predictable spaces. It&#8217;s about forgetting all the rules that we usually abide by when we&#8217;re interviewing school board officials and politicians and recognizing that when it comes to trauma, we need to be treating our interview subjects differently.&#8221;</p>



<p>Discussions about trauma-informed journalism are becoming more common due to a confluence of factors, including the COVID-19 pandemic and an increased hostility toward journalists. Together, these elements &#8220;have really made it clear that for journalists to do their jobs, they need to understand trauma more, so that they can tell better stories,&#8221; says <a href="https://dartcenter.org/about/people/elana-newman" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Elana Newman</a>, research director at the Dart Center for Journalism &amp; Trauma at Columbia University and McFarlin Professor of Psychology at the University of Tulsa.</p>



<p>This explainer and tip sheet on trauma-informed journalism is based on a review of several reliable sources on trauma-informed reporting and interviews with Newman of the Dart Center, and Cherry, founder of Pickup Communications PR agency, which provides services and tools for victims and survivors of traumatic events, the journalists who cover their stories and the criminal justice sector. We use<a href="https://dartcenter.org/resources/dart-center-style-guide"> The Dart Center Style Guide for Trauma-Informed Journalism</a> as our source for defining trauma-related terminology.</p>



<blockquote class="wp-block-quote is-layout-flow wp-block-quote-is-layout-flow">
<p>&#8220;Trauma-informed journalism means understanding trauma, understanding what a trauma survivor is experiencing before you show up at their door, and understanding how your actions [as a journalist] will impact them after you pack up and leave.&#8221;</p>
<cite>Tamara Cherry</cite></blockquote>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading"><strong>1. Understand that trauma impacts the brain, including the memory and the ability to vocalize events as they happened.</strong></h3>



<p>Trauma results from an event, series of events, or set of circumstances that are physically or emotionally harmful or life threatening to an individual, and have &#8220;lasting adverse effects on the individual’s functioning and mental, physical, social, emotional, or spiritual well-being,&#8221; according to the <a href="https://www.samhsa.gov/trauma-violence" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">U.S. Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration</a>.</p>



<p>Trauma can significantly affect a person&#8217;s perception and judgment. When faced with traumatic situations, the brain can go into survival mode and people can suddenly feel and behave differently from their normal selves. &#8220;That’s why eyewitnesses to traumatic events are notoriously unreliable. It’s also why even seasoned journalists at moments of high emotion can sometimes get it wrong,&#8221; according to the Dart Center&#8217;s &#8220;<a href="https://dartcenter.org/sites/default/files/DCE_JournoTraumaHandbook.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Trauma &amp; Journalism: A Guide for Journalists, Editors &amp; Managers</a>.&#8221;</p>



<p>Trauma impacts the brain, memories and a person&#8217;s ability to vocalize their stories, says Cherry. Immediately after a tragedy, &#8220;we as journalists are asking people to tell their stories, when quite often they literally can&#8217;t,&#8221; she says. &#8220;So, we might think that we&#8217;re getting their story, but really, we&#8217;re getting whatever version of their story that their brain is coming up with in the moment.&#8221;&nbsp;</p>



<p>But months down the road, when the sources have had time to heal a little and they&#8217;re not in <a href="https://www.health.harvard.edu/staying-healthy/understanding-the-stress-response">fight or flight</a> mode, we might actually get a more accurate story, Cherry says.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading"><strong>2. When getting informed consent to tell a trauma survivor’s story, err on the side of overexplaining.</strong></h3>



<p>In the context of trauma-informed reporting, informed consent means informing your sources about the nature of your story and having their consent to use their name and report on their experiences. While this is an important aspect of reporting many stories, trauma-informed reporting requires journalists to err on the side of overexplaining. &nbsp;</p>



<p>&#8220;Don’t assume [sources] know what terms like ‘on the record’ or ‘on background’ mean. If that’s something you’re discussing, make sure to be extremely clear about how they will or won’t be identified,&#8221; writes journalist <a href="https://twitter.com/Alice_Wilder">Alice Wilder</a> in &#8220;<a href="https://transom.org/2021/trauma-informed-reporting/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Trauma Informed Reporting</a>,&#8221; published for <a href="http://transom.org/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Transom</a>, a nonprofit organization that brings new voices and ideas to public broadcasting via workshops and its website.</p>



<p>Give your sources the option to review their quotes or even to change their mind about being quoted before a story is published.</p>



<p>In writing her book, Cherry allowed the people she surveyed and interviewed &#8212; including more than 100 trauma survivors &#8212; to review the parts in which they were quoted or where their stories were used to ensure their continued consent and accuracy of the survivors’ accounts after her initial interview.</p>



<p>&#8220;I knew enough about trauma to really appreciate the weight of responsibility that I had to get this right and to not cause further harm,” she says. “I wanted them to be able to review their excerpt, but I didn&#8217;t want it to leave them in a dark place. I wanted it to leave them in a better place.”</p>



<blockquote class="wp-block-quote is-layout-flow wp-block-quote-is-layout-flow">
<p>&#8220;Don’t assume [sources] know what terms like ‘on the record’ or ‘on background’ mean. If that’s something you’re discussing, make sure to be extremely clear about how they will or won’t be identified.&#8221;</p>
<cite>Alice Wilder</cite></blockquote>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading"><strong>3. Give power to survivors during the interview and storytelling process.</strong></h3>



<p>Journalists are trained not to let sources pick which questions they want to answer, see the questions ahead of the interview or the story before it&#8217;s published. But when it comes to interviewing survivors of trauma, it&#8217;s OK to break those rules. Let your sources know that they have control of the situation.</p>



<p>&#8220;One simple way to raise their comfort level is by not pushing any questions that they are not prepared to answer,&#8221; writes Maggie Doheny in her article &#8220;<a href="https://rjionline.org/news/best-practices-for-trauma-informed-journalism/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Best Practices for Trauma-Informed Journalism</a>,&#8221; published by the Reynolds Journalism Institute in December 2021.</p>



<p>The Dart Center&#8217;s guide, &#8220;<a href="https://dartcenter.org/content/tragedies-journalists-6?section=all" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Tragedies &amp; Journalists</a>,&#8221; has five tips for interviewing victims of trauma:</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li>Always treat victims with dignity and respect.</li>



<li>Clearly identify yourself.</li>



<li>Never say &#8220;I understand&#8221; or &#8220;I know how you feel.&#8221;</li>



<li>Don&#8217;t overwhelm your source with the hardest questions first.</li>



<li>If you receive a harsh reaction, leave a number or your card and explain that survivors can contact you if they want to talk later.</li>
</ul>



<p>In the aftermath of a tragedy, prepare yourself for a wide range of responses from survivors. &#8220;Bear in mind the emotional impact of what has happened. Approach people with care, respect and kindness. Take a moment to introduce yourself, make eye contact and explain why you would like to talk to them. Take it slowly and don’t rush &#8212; however chaotic the circumstances. Don’t just stuff a microphone in someone’s face and expect an interview,&#8221; according to the Dart Center&#8217;s <a href="https://dartcenter.org/sites/default/files/DCE_JournoTraumaHandbook.pdf">trauma and journalism guide</a>. &nbsp;</p>



<p>“Never ask that most overused and least effective of journalistic questions: ‘How do you feel?’ You may get tears in response, but you’re not likely to get a coherent, useful or meaningful answer. ‘How do you feel?’ is the one question survivors and victims consistently say they find the most distressing and inappropriate. Better options include: ‘How are you now?’ or ‘How did you experience that?’ or ‘What do you think about …?’” according to the Dart Center’s <a href="https://dartcenter.org/sites/default/files/DCE_JournoTraumaHandbook.pdf">trauma and journalism guide</a>. &nbsp;</p>



<p>&#8220;Opt for open-ended questions and let subjects know that they’re not required to answer questions,&#8221; advises Wilder in her <a href="https://transom.org/2021/trauma-informed-reporting/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Transom article</a>. &#8220;Instead of &#8216;Start at the beginning&#8217; or other chronological based questions ask, &#8216;Where would you like to begin?&#8217; or &#8216;Would you tell me what you are able to remember about your experience?'&#8221;</p>



<p>Wilder also advises against questioning&nbsp;why&nbsp;your source is emotional: &#8220;Even if the traumatic event was long ago, or if it doesn’t seem &#8216;that bad&#8217; to you, their reaction should be respected,&#8221; she write. She also advises against saying “I understand what you’re going through” or “I know how you feel.”</p>



<p>Ask survivors what they would like to achieve in telling their story, advises Cherry.</p>



<p>And if you&#8217;re producing a podcast, video, or a printed story about a crime or tragedy that happened in the past, make sure the survivors are aware of it and are extended the opportunity to include their voice and be part of the process. &#8220;Because if they&#8217;re not, then they could really feel like we are talking about them behind their back,&#8221; says Cherry. &#8220;Or they can be surprised by it because they might not even know [the podcast/show/story] is happening.&#8221;</p>



<blockquote class="wp-block-quote is-layout-flow wp-block-quote-is-layout-flow">
<p>&#8220;‘How do you feel?’ is the one question survivors and victims consistently say they find the most distressing and inappropriate. Better options include: ‘How are you now?’ or ‘How did you experience that?’ or ‘What do you think about …?'&#8221;</p>
<cite>Dart Center&#8217;s <a href="https://dartcenter.org/sites/default/files/DCE_JournoTraumaHandbook.pdf">Trauma &amp; Journalism: A Guide For Journalists, Editors &amp; Managers</a></cite></blockquote>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading"><strong>4. Have a plan for your interviews.</strong></h3>



<p>If you&#8217;re interviewing a trauma survivor, think about how you&#8217;re caring for them before, during and after the interview.</p>



<p>Let survivors pick the place of the interview, so they can feel safe. Tell survivors what questions you&#8217;re planning to ask, Cherry writes in her <a href="https://www.pickupcommunications.com/tips" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">tip sheet</a>. Ask if there are questions you should avoid. </p>



<p>During the interview, make sure survivors aren&#8217;t reliving the event. Find out if they want to take a break. </p>



<p>At then end of the interview, thank your sources and let them how their interview will be used and when to expect the story, advises journalist and author Jo Healey in &#8220;<a href="https://dartcenter.org/resources/reporting-coronavirus-handling-sensitive-remote-interviews" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Reporting on Coronavirus: Handling Sensitive Remote Interviews</a>,&#8221; published in 2020 on the Dart Center&#8217;s website.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading"><strong>5. Don&#8217;t forget about your own mental well-being.</strong></h3>



<figure class="wp-block-embed alignright is-type-wp-embed is-provider-the-journalist-039-s-resource wp-block-embed-the-journalist-039-s-resource"><div class="wp-block-embed__wrapper">
<blockquote class="wp-embedded-content" data-secret="ndVreVh1wu"><a href="https://journalistsresource.org/home/self-care-tips-for-journalists-plus-a-list-of-several-resources/">Self-care tips for journalists &#8212; plus a list of several resources</a></blockquote><iframe loading="lazy" class="wp-embedded-content" sandbox="allow-scripts" security="restricted" style="position: absolute; clip: rect(1px, 1px, 1px, 1px);" title="&#8220;Self-care tips for journalists &#8212; plus a list of several resources&#8221; &#8212; The Journalist&#039;s Resource" src="https://journalistsresource.org/home/self-care-tips-for-journalists-plus-a-list-of-several-resources/embed/#?secret=rhwi6bpwc7#?secret=ndVreVh1wu" data-secret="ndVreVh1wu" width="600" height="338" frameborder="0" marginwidth="0" marginheight="0" scrolling="no"></iframe>
</div></figure>



<p>Over the years, research has shown that journalists&#8217; job can <a href="https://journalistsresource.org/environment/job-stress-journalists-health-research/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">affect their mental health</a>.</p>



<p>Depending on their beats or work locations,&nbsp;<a href="https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/epdf/10.1023/A%3A1024405716529" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">4%</a>&nbsp;to&nbsp;<a href="https://dartcenter.org/content/covering-trauma-impact-on-journalists" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">59%</a>&nbsp;of journalists have symptoms of post-traumatic stress disorder, or PTSD, according to the&nbsp;<a href="https://dartcenter.org/content/covering-trauma-impact-on-journalists" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Dart Center</a>.</p>



<p>Journalists should be aware of signs of trouble, which include inability to concentrate, feeling on edge or numb, or inability to feel compassion for sources, Newman explained in <a href="https://journalistsresource.org/home/self-care-tips-for-journalists-plus-a-list-of-several-resources/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">a 2021</a>. Other signs include inability to sleep, feeling angry, or excessive use of alcohol.</p>



<p>Here are five self-care tips from the Dart Center&#8217;s 2009 guide, &#8220;<a href="https://dartcenter.org/content/tragedies-journalists-6?section=all" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Tragedies &amp; Journalists</a>&#8220;:</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li>Know your limits.</li>



<li>Take breaks.</li>



<li>Find a friend or colleague who is a good listener.</li>



<li>Find a hobby, exercise, spend time with family and friends.</li>



<li>Seek counseling if you feel overwhelmed.</li>
</ul>



<p>Newman also shared self-care tips for journalists in 2021, which we summarized in &#8220;<a href="https://journalistsresource.org/home/self-care-tips-for-journalists-plus-a-list-of-several-resources/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Self-care tips for journalists &#8212; plus a list of several resources</a>.&#8221;</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading"><strong>Recommended reading</strong></h3>



<figure class="wp-block-embed alignright is-type-wp-embed is-provider-the-journalist-039-s-resource wp-block-embed-the-journalist-039-s-resource"><div class="wp-block-embed__wrapper">
<blockquote class="wp-embedded-content" data-secret="HzTVuvIvcN"><a href="https://journalistsresource.org/home/journalism-stress-solutions/">Journalists are under stress. What&#8217;s the solution?</a></blockquote><iframe loading="lazy" class="wp-embedded-content" sandbox="allow-scripts" security="restricted" style="position: absolute; clip: rect(1px, 1px, 1px, 1px);" title="&#8220;Journalists are under stress. What&#8217;s the solution?&#8221; &#8212; The Journalist&#039;s Resource" src="https://journalistsresource.org/home/journalism-stress-solutions/embed/#?secret=w6iYFevJ9x#?secret=HzTVuvIvcN" data-secret="HzTVuvIvcN" width="600" height="338" frameborder="0" marginwidth="0" marginheight="0" scrolling="no"></iframe>
</div></figure>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li>&#8220;<a href="https://dartcenter.org/resources/dart-center-style-guide">The Dart Center Style Guide for Trauma-Informed Journalism</a>,&#8221; Dart Center for Journalism &amp; Trauma, June 2021.</li>

Dart Center, February 2009.</li>

Dart Center for Journalism &amp; Trauma, November 2019.</li>

Donald W. Reynolds Journalism Institute at the University of Missouri, December 2021.</li>

Center for Health Journalism.</li>

Dart Center, April 2020.</li>

Government</a></span></div><h3 class="entry-title"><a href="https://journalistsresource.org/economics/iran-war-economic-consequences/">4 takeaways on the economic consequences of the Iran war</a></h3><div class="entry-meta"><span class="cat-post-date post-date">  March 20, 2026</span></div></header><div class="entry-content"><p class="entry-content">Economic uncertainty, windfalls for oil producers, how businesses communicate with the president and artificial intelligence — check out the insights from our webinar with EconoFact. 
</p></div></div></div></article><article class="cat-post-item has-post-thumbnail"><div class="jr-post-intro-container  jr-sidebar-post-item"><div class="jr-sidebar-post-img"><a href="https://journalistsresource.org/home/childhood-vaccines-what-the-research-says-about-their-safety-and-side-effects/" title="Childhood vaccines: What research shows about their safety and potential side effects"><img width="728" height="484" src="https://journalistsresource.org/wp-content/uploads/2025/02/faith-043-728x484.jpg" class="lcp_thumbnail wp-post-image" alt="Childhood vaccines: What research shows about their safety and potential side effects" decoding="async" loading="lazy" /></a></a></div><div class="jr-post-intro-text"><header class="entry-header"><div class="entry-category-meta"><span class="cat-links"><a href="https://journalistsresource.org/health/" rel="category tag">Health</a>, <a href="https://journalistsresource.org/politics-and-government/" rel="category tag">Politics &amp; Government</a></span></div><h3 class="entry-title"><a href="https://journalistsresource.org/home/childhood-vaccines-what-the-research-says-about-their-safety-and-side-effects/">Childhood vaccines: What research shows about their safety and potential side effects</a></h3><div class="entry-meta"><span class="cat-post-date post-date">  January 5, 2026</span></div></header><div class="entry-content"><p class="entry-content">In this piece, we share reporting tips, explain how vaccine side effects are tracked in the U.S., and discuss research on the safety of childhood vaccines.
</p></div></div></div></article><div class="jr-expert-commentary-textlabel"><p><strong>Expert Commentary</strong></p></div><article class="cat-post-item has-post-thumbnail expert-commentary"><div class="jr-post-intro-container  jr-sidebar-post-item"><div class="jr-sidebar-post-img"><a href="https://journalistsresource.org/politics-and-government/287g-the-program-that-lets-state-and-local-police-perform-the-functions-of-federal-immigration-officers/" title="287(g): The program that lets state and local police perform the functions of federal immigration officers"><img width="728" height="484" src="https://journalistsresource.org/wp-content/uploads/2025/04/54307135384_dc6f645841_k-728x484.jpg" class="lcp_thumbnail wp-post-image" alt="287(g): The program that lets state and local police perform the functions of federal immigration officers" decoding="async" loading="lazy" /></a></a></div><div class="jr-post-intro-text"><header class="entry-header"><div class="entry-category-meta"><span class="cat-links"><a href="https://journalistsresource.org/criminal-justice/" rel="category tag">Criminal Justice</a>, <a href="https://journalistsresource.org/politics-and-government/" rel="category tag">Politics &amp; Government</a></span></div><h3 class="entry-title"><a href="https://journalistsresource.org/politics-and-government/287g-the-program-that-lets-state-and-local-police-perform-the-functions-of-federal-immigration-officers/">287(g): The program that lets state and local police perform the functions of federal immigration officers</a></h3><div class="entry-meta"><span class="cat-post-date post-date">  April 30, 2025</span></div></header><div class="entry-content"><p class="entry-content">&#8220;In the span of about two months, the Trump administration radically expanded the 287(g) program beyond anything I have seen in the past 15 years of close study of this precise policy,&#8221; writes immigration scholar Austin Kocher.
</p></div></div></div></article></div></aside>

</div><!-- #secondary -->

<script type="text/javascript">
		document.addEventListener("DOMContentLoaded", function() {
			var if_ssb_exist = document.getElementsByClassName( "simplesocialbuttons" ).length > 0;
			if (if_ssb_exist) {
				var ssb_admin_ajax = 'https://journalistsresource.org/wp-admin/admin-ajax.php';
				var ssb_post_id = 70550 ;
				var ssb_post_url = 'https://journalistsresource.org/home/trauma-informed-journalism-explainer/';
				var ssb_alternate_post_url = 'http://journalistsresource.org/home/trauma-informed-journalism-explainer/';
				jQuery( document ).ready(function(){
				var is_ssb_used = jQuery('.simplesocialbuttons');
				if( is_ssb_used ) {

					var data = {
					'action'   : 'ssb_fetch_data',
					'postID'   :  ssb_post_id,
					'security' : '3d662304a5'
					};
					jQuery.post(ssb_admin_ajax, data, function(data, textStatus, xhr) {
						var array = JSON.parse(data);

						jQuery.each( array, function( index, value ){

							if( index == 'total' ){
								jQuery('.ssb_'+ index +'_counter').html(value + '<span>Shares</span>');
							}else{
								jQuery('.ssb_'+ index +'_counter').html(value);
							}
						});

					});
				}
				})

			//ssbPlugin.fetchFacebookShares();
			}
		});
